Daily Galaxy
Scientists find black hole disks could build objects 1,000 times the mass of Earth from tiny dust grains
Story by Sarah Jones • 3w • 3 min read


Credit: ESO | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
Credit: ESO | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
© Daily Galaxy US
Dust swirling around feeding supermassive black holes may be doing something unexpected: building planet-sized objects. Computer simulations suggest a single active galactic nucleus (AGN) disk could contain tens of millions of them.

Astronomers from New Mexico State University, the Nicolaus Copernicus Astronomical Center and CUNY Borough of Manhattan Community College studied the dusty tori around AGN, the bright galactic cores powered by supermassive black holes pulling in material. They wanted to know whether some of the same processes that build planets around young stars could also work in this much harsher environment.

Salem U Biology in Physiology - Affordable In-State Tuition - Prepare for Further Study
Salem U Biology in Physiology - Affordable In-State Tuition - Prepare for Further Study
Salem University
Salem University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Wladimir Lyra and his colleagues found some surprising similarities. Their models show that tiny dust grains can clump together, become concentrated through streaming instability and eventually collapse under their own gravity. And these grains really are tiny: they start out at sizes ranging from just a few nanometers to a fraction of a millimeter.

Tiny Dust Can Build Planet-Mass Objects
The researchers modeled a strongly magnetized AGN disk containing dust drifting in from the interstellar medium. The individual grains range from only afew nanometers to a fraction of a millimeter. Under the conditions simulated by the team, those tiny particles can coagulate and become concentrated instead of remaining dispersed through the disk.

The process is driven by streaming instability, which concentrates dust into dense filaments. Once sufficiently concentrated, these structures collapse under their own gravity, seeding objects ranging from Earth-mass bodies to super-Jupiters.

“We’re finding objects that are a thousand times the mass of the Earth, but built of pure dust,” Wladimir Lyra, an astrophysicist at New Mexico State University, said. “And not only that, but also some of these objects are approaching the mass of the Sun.”


Dust grain growth and timescales across AGN disks for different supermassive black hole masses. Credit: The Astrophysical Journal
Dust grain growth and timescales across AGN disks for different supermassive black hole masses. Credit: The Astrophysical Journal
© Daily Galaxy US
The study, published inThe Astrophysical Journal, estimates that such disks could host on the order of tens of millions of planetary-mass objects. Growth can continue through pebble accretion and gas accretion over the roughly 1-to-10-million-year lifetime of an AGN episode.

0
Some Dust-built Objects Can Become Stars
Continued growth can take some of these objects beyond the planetary regime. The simulations indicate that certain bodies can approach or pass the hydrogen-burning limit, the threshold at which an object becomes massive enough to become a star.

That is very different from the way Lyra describes normal star formation. Usually, the story starts with a large, dense cloud of gas that becomes unstable and collapses under its own gravity. The process runs the other way around. Small building blocks come first, then grow larger by gathering more material.

“Our mechanism is the opposite,” Lyra explained. “You form from the bottom up. You first form the building blocks and then accrete gas and then boom, you form a star.”

Tiny grains can therefore assemble into larger bodies before continued gas accretion pushes some of them across the hydrogen-burning threshold.

“This is a mechanism of forming stars that we discovered for the first time,” Lyra said.

The Same Disks Could Build Massive Black Holes
The chain does not necessarily stop at stars. Stars formed inside an AGN disk could eventually collapse into black holes. Meanwhile, low-mass black holes already moving through the disk could behave somewhat like young planets still forming around a star.Lyra noted these black holes could migrate through the disk, change their orbits and collide with other objects, creating increasingly massive black holes. The researchers have developed this idea into what is known as the “AGN channel.”

“That’s a completely different way to form heavy black holes than anything else in the Universe,” he added.


Models of object growth across AGN disks around supermassive black holes. Credit: The Astrophysical Journal
Models of object growth across AGN disks around supermassive black holes. Credit: The Astrophysical Journal
© Daily Galaxy US
Some of those black holes could become huge. Bhupendra Mishra said they could reach hundreds or thousands of times the mass of the Sun. If they begin moving toward the supermassive black hole at the center, they could also generate gravitational-wave signals. Those signals could potentially be picked up by ESA’s Laser Interferometer Space Antenna (LISA), which is expected to launch in the mid-2030s.

“They’re hundreds or thousands of times the size of the Sun, and if they start to move towards the center, they also produce a signal which will be detected probably by LISA,” Mishra said.

McDonald's didn't lose lawsuit against chef Jamie Oliver over 'unfit' food claims
Story by Laerke Christensen • 1mo • 7 min read


A composite image shows British TV Chef Jamie Oliver and the McDonald's golden arches. Neil P. Mockford, accessed via Getty Images, Andre Carrotflower, accessed via WikiCommons, illustrated by Snopes
A composite image shows British TV Chef Jamie Oliver and the McDonald's golden arches. Neil P. Mockford, accessed via Getty Images, Andre Carrotflower, accessed via WikiCommons, illustrated by Snopes

In July 2026, a claim spread that McDonald's lost a lawsuit against the TV chef Jamie Oliver, who — according to posts — proved that the fast food chain's food was "unfit for human consumption" because it was "highly toxic." The claim is unproven. 
Snopes previously investigated a variation of this claim in 2018 and 2026. We found then and now that Oliver said in a 2011 TV program that beef trimmings used for a product he called "pink slime" were "not fit for human consumption." We found no evidence that he specifically called McDonald's food "unfit for human consumption" or "toxic," as the posts suggested.
The American Meat Institute, a trade association that represents processors of meat and poultry, said in 2012 that beef trimmings used to make "pink slime," which it calls Boneless Lean Beef Trimmings, were edible.
However, because we could not access a full version of the "Jamie Oliver's Food Revolution" episode where Oliver discussed "pink slime" to see if he specifically mentioned McDonald's, we leave this claim unrated.

Snopes will update this report if, or when, additional information surfaces. We contacted Oliver and McDonald's to ask whether Oliver has ever called the fast food chain's food "unfit for human consumption" or "toxic" or whether McDonald's ever lost a lawsuit involving Oliver and are waiting for responses.
A  claim (archived) circulated online in July 2026 that McDonald's lost a lawsuit against the TV chef Jamie Oliver after Oliver proved its food was "unfit for human consumption" because it was "highly toxic."

The claim spread primarily on Facebook, where one user wrote:

Expand article logo  Continue reading

McDonald's loses lawsuit against chef Jamie Oliver, who proved that the food they sell is unfit for human consumption because it is highly toxic.

Oliver demonstrates how hamburgers are made. According to Oliver, fatty cuts of meat are "washed" with ammonium hydroxide and then used to fill the hamburger patties. Even before this process, the TV presenter says, this meat was unfit for human consumption.

Oliver, a radical activist chef taking on the food industry, says: "We are talking about meat that is sold as dog food and then served to humans. Aside from the quality of the meat, ammonium hydroxide is harmful to health." Oliver calls it "the pink slime process."

Could the universe have existed forever?
Story by Dylan Moore • 1d
Could the Universe Have Existed Forever
©Photo by NASA Hubble Space Telescope on Unsplash
The Big Bang remains the leading scientific model for describing the early evolution of the observable universe, but cosmologists continue to explore whether the universe necessarily had an absolute beginning. A 2025 physics paper examined mathematical models describing universes that could be eternal in both the past and future.

The research does not overturn the Big Bang model or prove that the universe has always existed. Instead, it investigates whether certain cosmological models can remain complete without requiring a traditional beginning, raising difficult questions about time, gravity, and the universe itself.

Metro
We’ve just realised our understanding of the universe might be wrong
Story by Josh Milton • 1mo • 3 min read


We’ve thought this one thing about the universe for 100 years (Picture: Getty/Metro)
We’ve thought this one thing about the universe for 100 years (Picture: Getty/Metro)
The universe, that 13.8billion-year-old thing we call home, is a weird place.

But one thing that has made sense to scientists for the better part of a century is the belief that the cosmos is surprisingly uniform.

This is known as the cosmological principle, or the idea that if you took a step back – a very big step back – and took a photo of the universe, it would look smooth and blurry, like you forgot to wear glasses.

Physiology & Exercise Con. - Salem U Biology in Physiology - Prepare for Further Study
Physiology & Exercise Con. - Salem U Biology in Physiology - Prepare for Further Study
Salem University
Salem University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
A paper published in the journal Nature, however, argues this might not be the case.

Rather, things look different from every direction you look at from a large scale, and the galaxies line up almost into patterns.

While this might not sound like a big deal, if proven, this would change the maths that explains how existence works.

Researchers figured this out by looking at the largest map of the known universe observed by the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument.


A slice of the largest map of the universe (Picture: Collaboration/DOE/KPNO/NOIR)
A slice of the largest map of the universe (Picture: Collaboration/DOE/KPNO/NOIR)
They compared this to simulations of how dark matter – the mysterious something holding galaxies together – is distributed in a uniform cosmos.

Researchers checked if there was any kind of pattern when comparing them.

‘Essentially, we asked what the probability of finding a galaxy at a given distance with respect to another and along a given direction,’ study co-author Marco Galoppo tells Metro.

Related video: A radical alternative to the Big Bang is raising alarming questions (Celestium)

Celestium
A radical alternative to the Big Bang is raising alarming questions
One night in 1946, three lecturer friends at Cambridge University
Galoppo, of the University of Canterbury in New Zealand, should have been left cooing at how homogeneous (the same everywhere) and isotropic (the same in all directions) the universe is.

It should begin to smooth out when observed at scales of about 100 megaparsecs – roughly 300 million light-years – from every direction.

Instead, they saw a stringy, bumpy web of galaxies and voids even when approaching a whopping one gigaparsec, which is three billion light-years.

Follow Metro on WhatsApp to be the first to get all the latest news

Follow us to receive the latest news updates from Metro (Picture: Getty Images)
Follow us to receive the latest news updates from Metro (Picture: Getty Images)
© Provided by Metro
Metro’s on Whatsapp! Join our community for breaking news and juicy stories.

‘We would all (where “all” is the observational cosmology community of 5,000 people) love this mismatch to be genuinely new physics because for us that is like winning the World Cup,’ she adds.

‘But my personal opinion, this study is part of the qualifying stages, not the final 32.’

Rowan University - Earn A Biology Degree Online - Research Opportunities
Rowan University - Earn A Biology Degree Online - Research Opportunities
Rowan University
Rowan University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Konstantinos Migkas, a Dutch astrophysicist, says the findings follow similar studies published this year.

And his own, too, which was referenced by Galoppo. Migkas and his colleagues also found signs of anisotropy, adding further questions to why there is something in some places but not elsewhere.

‘This does not mean that anything has been concluded yet, but it is natural that assumptions built into our standard model will be tested more sharply as data quality and volume improve,’ Migkas tells Metro.

Not everyone is swayed.

‘It’s not a big deal for the world of cosmology because this paper is wrong,’ says Seshadri Nadathur, a professor in cosmology at the University of Portsmouth.

Nadathur suggested the patterns could be chalked up to galaxy bias, where galaxies seem to clump together, which distorts measurements.

‘The reason we believe the universe is uniform is because observations of the real universe suggest it is true, and there has never been any convincing evidence otherwise,’ he adds.

‘There are occasional claims of some discovery contradicting this picture, but they are always flawed.’

Story of black bear taking wounded animals to wildlife center is too good to be true
Story by Emery Winter • 3d • 5 min read


A screenshot of pair of AI-generated images stacked vertically together, the top one showing a black bear carrying a raccoon to the back of a building and the bottom one of a black bear carrying a fox to the back of a very similar, but not exactly the same building. Snopes' red 
A screenshot of pair of AI-generated images stacked vertically together, the top one showing a black bear carrying a raccoon to the back of a building and the bottom one of a black bear carrying a fox to the back of a very similar, but not exactly the same building. Snopes' red

Claim:

As shared in September 2026 social media posts, a black bear took a raccoon and later a fox to a wildlife center where it had previously been treated.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

In September 2026, a rumor circulated online about a black bear that apparently took a wounded raccoon, and later a wounded fox, to a wildlife center where the bear itself had been treated.

For example, one Facebook post (archived) said:

The first time, nobody believed it. Security cameras at a wildlife center captured a black bear approaching a rear entrance carrying a wounded raccoon in its mouth. It placed the animal gently beside the door. Then disappeared into the woods.

[…]

Several weeks later, cameras recorded a bear approaching a side entrance with an injured fox. Same careful approach. Same gentle placement near the door. Same quiet exit into the trees.

Both animals were found alive and received treatment.

Workers compared the two recordings. Both bears had the same unusual white mark on their chest. Same bear. Two deliveries. Two injured animals brought to the one place that could help them.

Staff searched old photographs and records of bears previously treated at the facility. That's when everything clicked.

Years earlier, the same bear had been rescued, treated at that very wildlife center, and released back into the wild. He had been a patient there. And now he was bringing patients back.

Officials believe the bear remembered where it had once been helped and returned because it somehow understood that injured animals would be safe there.



Other Facebook (archived) posts (archived) shared the same text. The black bear story also appeared in a YouTube short (archived).

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Snopes readers sent emails and searched the website wondering whether the story was true.

There's no evidence supporting the rumor that a black bear took a wounded raccoon and a fox to a wildlife center it had previously received treatment at. It was posted online by a page with a history of posting heartwarming — though fictitious — animal stories generated with artificial intelligence tools.

Nasa creates a ‘map’ of the Universe by making a composite of images from the Hubble Telescope
Oliver Wheaton
Oliver Wheaton | News writer for Metro
Published January 22, 2015 2:56pm Updated December 11, 2019 10:26am
Comments

Say hello to our neighbours (Picture: Nasa)
This image is the closest thing we have ever had to a map of the Universe.

Or at least part of it, namely the Andromeda Galaxy, the Milky Way’s closest neighbour (which sits two million light years away from us).

Nasa shared this composite of images taken by the Hubble Space Telescope, that has finally allowed them to identify and mark out individual stars and constellations in a single image.

While it may be pretty to look at, keep in mind that this took Nasa scientists years of work to create, and they compared the immense achievement to ‘photographing a beach and resolving individual grains of sand’.

symbol
00:00

00:00
Read More

MORE: Nasa spots huge black mark spreading over the sun


Your great ,great, great, great grandchildren might actually be able to visit these places, possibly (Picture: Nasa)
MORE: ‘UFOs’ spotted in the skies 666 miles from Roswell as people report alien sightings

Not only is this an image of space, but also of time, as 61,000 lights years of space can be seen in this picture – meaning some of the cosmic events included in the composite occurred before modern humans even existed.

The composite is made up of 1.5 billion pixels, and they even created a version you can zoom in on (but don’t get your hopes up – you can’t see any alien houses on there), as well as a video which goes some of the way to explain how massive and complex their composite is.

This unexplained glow could unlock the universe’s biggest secret
Sarah Hooper
Sarah Hooper | Live News Reporter
Published October 17, 2025 3:58pm Updated October 17, 2025 3:59pm
Comments
France. Marne. Haute-Marne. The lac du Der during autumn. The Milky Way.
Mysterious glowing from the centre of our galaxy could be something even more outlandish (Picture: Getty)
The heart of the Milky Way could be holding the key to one of the universe’s oldest secrets.

A mysterious glow of gamma rays in the centre of our galaxy has been a mystery for decades, but researchers think they’ve found the reason for it.

Joseph Silk, a professor of physics and astronomy at Johns Hopkins, believes the elusive glow could be proof of dark matter colliding.

If true, it would be the first-ever proof that dark matter – the groovy, invisible glue that keeps galaxies together – exists.

symbol
00:00

00:00
Read More

‘Dark matter dominates the universe and holds galaxies together,’ Dr Silk said.

‘It’s extremely consequential and we’re desperately thinking all the time of ideas as to how we could detect it.’

A meteor shoots across the night sky sky leaving a trail of light across the milky way
The new study shows evidence that Gamma rays could be emitting from dark matter (Picture: Getty)
The new study, headed up by Dr Silk, saw scientists make a map of where they think dark matter is in the galaxy.

The maps show evidence that the gamma ray glows emitting from the centre of our galaxy could be caused by this dark matter.


It could also be old neutron stars, which are rewoken, that are causing this glow. But that’s more unlikely.

‘A clean signal would be a smoking gun, in my opinion,’ Dr Silk said.

‘It’s possible we will see the new data and confirm one theory over the other. Or maybe we’ll find nothing, in which case it’ll be an even greater mystery to resolve.’

Earlier this year, breathtaking images of thousands of galaxies taken by a 609-megapixel camera were released by space officials.

The Milky Way and night sky over St Cwyfans Church at night on Anglesey. (Photo by: Alan Novelli/Loop Images/Universal Images Group via Getty Images)
The Milky Way holds a lot of secrets (Picture: Alan Novelli)
The images were taken by Euclid, a European Space Agency (ESA) telescope launched in 2023 that hopes to capture the ‘dark universe’.

‘Not only are these images pretty, they’re delivering on the science,’ Adam Amara, chief scientist at the UK Space Agency, who first proposed the idea for Euclid, told Metro.

Astronomers are also intrigued by what’s not visible in the images: dark matter. Researchers have long looked into dark matter to explain why the universe is the way it is, thinking of it as gravitational scaffolding.

Trending Now

Husband electrocuted 'trying to rescue wife from hot tub' as horrific details emerge
News
15 hours ago
By Emily Bashforth
The 9/11 Falling Man: The story behind an image that shocked the world
The haunting story of the 'waving woman' seen at North Tower impact site
Girl, 15, and woman arrested after wrong-way crash on M1 leaves man in hospital
However, the aloof material has yet to be directly detected, making it one of the biggest challenges in modern physics.

‘When you’re a teenager, you learn all the science from textbooks and become a bit cocky,’ Amara said. ‘You learn all this fancy, brilliant stuff and then you hit dark matter and dark energy and you’re humbled.

‘In terms of the physics and the fundamentals of reality, we understand 5% of it all. Human knowledge explains just 5% of what’s out there.

‘Dark matter does stuff. It moves stars. It moves galaxies. It bends flight. We know it’s out there, but we have no idea what it is. We have a long way to go.’

Get in touch with our news team by emailing us at webnews@metro.co.uk.

Our Staff
Seshadri Nadathur
Seshadri Nadathur Portrait
Media ready expert
Professor Seshadri Nadathur
Professor of Cosmology

Seshadri.Nadathur@port.ac.uk

PhD Supervisor

View my research outputs
Summary
I am Professor of Cosmology at the Institute of Cosmology and Gravitation. My research area is observational cosmology – this means I study the properties of dark energy, dark matter and gravity, using the large-scale structure of the Universe and based on galaxy survey data collected by the leading ground-based and space telescopes.

My research has been funded by the Science and Technology Facilities Council (STFC), the UK Space Agency (UKSA), and the European Commission.

Research interests
I study the large-scale structure of the Universe, analysing the statistical patterns in the distribution of millions of galaxies to uncover cosmological information. I am particularly known for my work on baryon acoustic oscillations and the development of new methods beyond two-point clustering statistics, such as cosmic voids or density-split statistics. More generally I have worked on a wide range of other areas in cosmology, including redshift-space distortions, clustering of the Lyman-alpha forest and gravitational lensing, as well as theoretical models of dark matter, dark energy and inflation.  

A large fraction of my work involves the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument survey, or DESI. DESI is a 8-year spectroscopic survey programme to map the positions of 60 million galaxies and quasars in order to determine the dark energy equation of state, the neutrino mass scale, and a host of other science results. I led the Galaxy and Quasar Clustering working group in DESI from 2022-2025, when we published our major results from the first two data releases.

Another large part of my work is within the Euclid Consortium. Euclid is a space telescope, which combines spectroscopic measurements of around 30 million galaxies with weak lensing and photometric surveys of over a billion. I lead a UKSA-funded group at Portsmouth creating the spectroscopic galaxy catalogues and visibility mask for Euclid clustering science, and coordinate a DR1 Key Project on measuring baryon acoustic oscillations.

In the past I also worked on the extended Baryon Oscillation Spectroscopic Survey (eBOSS) and the Dark Energy Survey (DES).

Media availability
I am available for media requests, interviews and comments on astronomy- and cosmology-related stories. My previous contributions include appearances on The Sky At Night, a BBC4 documentary with Hannah Fry, and interviews for national and international media outlets.

I also give public lectures about cosmology in science festivals and meetings in the local area.

StarsInsider
The physics behind Einstein’s most famous equation
Story by Stars Insider • 6mo
The physics behind Einstein’s most famous equation
©Getty Images
During the cold autumnal months of 1905, German theoretical physicist Albert Einstein published a scientific paper that spoke about the properties of light and time. In the paper, he also included an equation that would come to be one of the most iconic in history: E=mc².

The equation not only redefined humanity’s understanding of energy and matter, but also changed how we think about time, motion, and the structure of the universe itself. For those dreaming of starships and intergalactic travel, E=mc² is both an invitation to explore and a door slammed shut.

So, what does it all mean? And what does it say about our future? Click through this gallery to find out.

Story of black bear taking wounded animals to wildlife center is too good to be true
Story by Emery Winter • 3d • 5 min read


A screenshot of pair of AI-generated images stacked vertically together, the top one showing a black bear carrying a raccoon to the back of a building and the bottom one of a black bear carrying a fox to the back of a very similar, but not exactly the same building. Snopes' red 
A screenshot of pair of AI-generated images stacked vertically together, the top one showing a black bear carrying a raccoon to the back of a building and the bottom one of a black bear carrying a fox to the back of a very similar, but not exactly the same building. Snopes' red

Claim:

As shared in September 2026 social media posts, a black bear took a raccoon and later a fox to a wildlife center where it had previously been treated.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

In September 2026, a rumor circulated online about a black bear that apparently took a wounded raccoon, and later a wounded fox, to a wildlife center where the bear itself had been treated.

For example, one Facebook post (archived) said:

The first time, nobody believed it. Security cameras at a wildlife center captured a black bear approaching a rear entrance carrying a wounded raccoon in its mouth. It placed the animal gently beside the door. Then disappeared into the woods.

[…]

Several weeks later, cameras recorded a bear approaching a side entrance with an injured fox. Same careful approach. Same gentle placement near the door. Same quiet exit into the trees.

Both animals were found alive and received treatment.

Workers compared the two recordings. Both bears had the same unusual white mark on their chest. Same bear. Two deliveries. Two injured animals brought to the one place that could help them.

Staff searched old photographs and records of bears previously treated at the facility. That's when everything clicked.

Years earlier, the same bear had been rescued, treated at that very wildlife center, and released back into the wild. He had been a patient there. And now he was bringing patients back.

Officials believe the bear remembered where it had once been helped and returned because it somehow understood that injured animals would be safe there.



Other Facebook (archived) posts (archived) shared the same text. The black bear story also appeared in a YouTube short (archived).

Expand article logo  Continue reading

Snopes readers sent emails and searched the website wondering whether the story was true.

There's no evidence supporting the rumor that a black bear took a wounded raccoon and a fox to a wildlife center it had previously received treatment at. It was posted online by a page with a history of posting heartwarming — though fictitious — animal stories generated with artificial intelligence tools.

Exact calculations sharpen view of atomic nuclei
Story by Science X staff • 2w • 3 min read


When atomic nuclei collide it triggers a scattering reaction that can be measured to calculate the size and density of the nuclei. Credit: Osaka Metropolitan University
When atomic nuclei collide it triggers a scattering reaction that can be measured to calculate the size and density of the nuclei. Credit: Osaka Metropolitan University
Every high-energy nuclear collision leaves behind a trail of clues about the structure of atomic nuclei. Deciphering those clues, however, depends on the accuracy of the underlying theory. Physicists at Osaka Metropolitan University have now performed a full calculation within Glauber theory, a cornerstone framework for describing high-energy nuclear collisions.

Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities
Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities
Rowan University
Rowan University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Subscribe to our newsletter for the latest sci-tech news updates.

By overcoming a computational challenge that has long forced researchers to rely on approximations to reduce computational demands, the team has shown that its full calculation can accurately reproduce experimental data and provide a reliable framework for predicting the outcomes of future experiments involving ordinary and exotic nuclei.

The study was published in Physical Review Letters on May 18 and Physical Review C on June 1. Physical Review Letters provides a brief overview of the main findings, while Physical Review C contains the complete paper with additional details, results and analysis.

Why the calculations are difficult
Atomic nuclei are too small, and many too short-lived, to observe directly, even with the most powerful microscopes. So, to probe nuclear structure, physicists accelerate one nucleus toward another at high speed and analyze how the particles scatter after the collision. By comparing the observed scattering pattern with theoretical models, scientists can infer otherwise invisible properties, such as a nucleus' size and density.

Related video: Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed (Sciencephile the AI)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Sciencephile the AI
Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed
Current Time 
0:01
/
Duration 
21:10
0
Accurately modeling these collisions, however, is far from simple.

"The concept known as Glauber theory has long been used to study what happens when atomic nuclei collide at high speeds," Wataru Horiuchi, associate professor at Osaka Metropolitan University's Graduate School of Science and lead author of the study, said.

"However, because the calculations are highly complex, the theory has often been studied using approximations."

Glauber theory describes a nuclear collision as a series of interactions between the individual protons and neutrons in the colliding nuclei. In principle, all possible multiple-scattering processes must be included in the calculation; however, the sheer number of possible interactions makes the full calculation challenging.

From approximation to full calculation
To overcome this obstacle, the team combined realistic quantum-mechanical models of nuclear structure with large-scale Monte Carlo calculations. For collisions involving protons, helium nuclei and carbon nuclei, their approach accounted for all orders of the multiple-scattering processes predicted by Glauber theory, rather than relying on the simplifying approximations traditionally used to make the calculations manageable.

Pathway to Pursue an MS - Salem U Biology in Physiology - First Step to Your Master's
Pathway to Pursue an MS - Salem U Biology in Physiology - First Step to Your Master's
Salem University
Salem University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
The results closely matched available experimental data, including recent high-precision measurements of carbon-12 collisions. This agreement demonstrates that the full Glauber calculation can accurately describe the observed behavior of high-energy nuclear collisions.

However, this raised another question: Is it always necessary to include every possible scattering interaction in the calculation?

Their precise calculation provided a benchmark for answering this question. Using a mathematical technique called cumulant expansion, the researchers showed that the first two terms of a mathematical expansion, known as cumulants, are sufficient to closely approximate the full calculation. This suggests that future analyses could ensure computational efficiency without sacrificing reliability.

By demonstrating that all-order Glauber theory calculations are both feasible and highly accurate, the study offers a more reliable theoretical benchmark for interpreting high-energy nuclear collisions.

"We successfully performed an exact calculation based on Glauber theory," Horiuchi said. "This approach not only provides a good explanation for existing experimental results but also enables us to predict the outcomes of future experiments."

More information: W. Horiuchi et al, Glauber-Theory Calculations of High-Energy Nuclear Scattering Observables Using Variational Monte Carlo Wave Functions, Physical Review Letters (2026). DOI: 10.1103/ppqx-yn59. On arXiv: arxiv.org/abs/2512.20095

W. Horiuchi et al, Glauber-theory analysis of nuclear reactions on a 12C target with variational Monte Carlo wave functions, Physical Review C (2026). DOI: 10.1103/gcbk-s7tc. On arXiv: arxiv.org/abs/2512.20100

Provided by Osaka Metropolitan University

Glauber-Theory Calculations of High-Energy Nuclear Scattering Observables Using Variational Monte Carlo Wave Functions
W. Horiuchi
1,2,3,*, Y. Suzuki
4,3, and R. B. Wiringa
5

PDF
Share
Phys. Rev. Lett. 136, 202501 – Published 18 May, 2026

DOI: https://doi.org/10.1103/ppqx-yn59

Export Citation
Article has an altmetric score of 31
44 total citations on Dimensions.

Show metrics
Abstract
Experiments using intermediate- to high-energy radioactive nuclear beams present numerous findings. Extracting important properties of physical observables relies on a firm theoretical analysis. Though Glauber theory is believed to work well, no convincing calculation has so far been done. We perform Glauber-theory calculations of both elastic differential cross sections and total reaction cross sections for 𝑝+ 
12
 C 
 ,  
12
 C 
 + 
12
 C 
 , and  
6
 He 
 + 
12
 C 
  systems. The wave functions of both  
6
 He 
  and  
12
 C 
  are generated by variational Monte Carlo calculations with spatial and spin-isospin correlations induced by realistic two- and three-nucleon potentials. Glauber’s phase-shift function is computed by Monte Carlo integration up to all orders of nucleon-nucleon multiple scatterings. We show an excellent performance of the Glauber description to the selected data on the above systems. We also find that the cumulant expansion of the phase-shift function converges rapidly up to the second order for the above systems. This finding will open up interesting applications for the analysis of high-energy nuclear experiments.

Nuclear many-body theory

Daily Galaxy

Scientists use supercomputer simulations to investigate what came before the Big Bang
Story by Lydia Amazouz • 2mo • 4 min read


Credit: Shutterstock | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
Credit: Shutterstock | The Daily Galaxy --Great Discoveries Channel
© Daily Galaxy US
For decades, one of the biggest questions in cosmology has remained frustratingly out of reach: what, if anything, existed before the Big Bang? Now, researchers are using advanced computational techniques based on Albert Einstein’s theory of general relativity to investigate regions of cosmic history that were once considered inaccessible. A major review published in Living Reviews in Relativity highlights how numerical relativity is becoming a powerful tool for probing the universe’s earliest moments and testing ideas that previously existed largely in the realm of speculation.

One Class Per Month Model - Exercise Science Degree - Study Physiology Online
One Class Per Month Model - Exercise Science Degree - Study Physiology Online
Salem University
Salem University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Why The Earliest Universe Has Been So Difficult To Study
The challenge begins with a fundamental limitation in modern cosmology. When scientists use Einstein’s equations to trace the universe backward in time, they eventually encounter a singularity, a point where density, temperature, and curvature become effectively infinite. At that boundary, the mathematical framework that successfully describes planets, stars, galaxies, and black holes stops providing meaningful answers. As a result, questions surrounding the origin of the universe have often remained beyond the reach of direct scientific testing.

Traditional cosmological models simplify the universe by assuming it is smooth and uniform on large scales. This approach has been remarkably successful in explaining many observations, including the large-scale structure of the cosmos and the properties of the cosmic microwave background. Yet conditions in the very early universe were likely far more chaotic. Gravity may have been operating in environments filled with extreme density fluctuations, violent distortions of spacetime, and highly uneven distributions of matter. Under such circumstances, the standard approximations begin to break down, leaving researchers with incomplete tools for understanding what truly happened near the beginning of cosmic history.

That limitation has fueled decades of debate over competing theories, including cosmic inflation, cyclic universes, bouncing cosmologies, and other models attempting to explain how the universe emerged into its current state.


Credit: Living Reviews in Relativity
Credit: Living Reviews in Relativity
© Daily Galaxy US
How Numerical Relativity Is Opening New Territory
The growing field of numerical relativity offers a way forward. Rather than relying solely on equations that can be solved analytically, researchers use powerful computers to approximate solutions in situations that are too complex for conventional mathematics. This technique has already transformed astrophysics by helping scientists model black hole mergers and predict the gravitational wave signals later detected by observatories such as LIGO.

Related video: Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed (Sciencephile the AI)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Sciencephile the AI
Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed
Hello mortals.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
21:10
0
The same computational strategy is now being applied to cosmology. Instead of assuming perfect symmetry, scientists can simulate highly irregular universes and observe how they evolve under the full framework of Einstein’s theory. This allows researchers to examine whether proposed models of the early universe remain stable under realistic conditions or collapse when subjected to the complexities of gravity.

As researcher David Lim explains, “You can search around the lamppost, but you can’t go far beyond the lamppost, where it’s dark—you just can’t solve those equations. Numerical relativity allows you to explore regions away from the lamppost.”

That analogy captures a central goal of the field. Scientists have long been able to investigate the parts of cosmology illuminated by manageable mathematics. Numerical relativity is enabling them to move beyond those limits and explore regions that were previously hidden from scientific inquiry.


We divide our review into work that focuses on the pre-Big Bang phase, which covers the period up to the end of inflation on this diagram. The post-Big Bang phase covers non-perturbative dynamics from the end of inflation to the emission of the CMB. The late-universe phase is the remainder of the diagram, which contains the standard cosmological history. Credit: Living Reviews in Relativity
We divide our review into work that focuses on the pre-Big Bang phase, which covers the period up to the end of inflation on this diagram. The post-Big Bang phase covers non-perturbative dynamics from the end of inflation to the emission of the CMB. The late-universe phase is the remainder of the diagram, which contains the standard cosmological history. Credit: Living Reviews in Relativity
© Daily Galaxy US
Testing Ideas About A Universe Before The Big Bang
One of the most intriguing possibilities involves theories suggesting thatthe Big Bang was not the absolute beginning of everything. Some models propose that the universe experiences recurring cycles of contraction and expansion. Others suggest that a previous cosmic phase collapsed before rebounding into the expanding universe observed today.

Online Courses For Adults
Online Courses For Adults
University of Phoenix
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
These concepts have often faced a major obstacle: proving whether they can actually work under realistic physical conditions. A theory may appear elegant on paper while failing when gravity, inhomogeneities, and relativistic effects are fully included. Numerical relativity provides a way to perform those tests.

According to the review published inLiving Reviews in Relativity, these simulations may help determine whether bounce scenarios remain mathematically stable, whether inflation can emerge naturally from chaotic initial conditions, and whether alternative origin theories leave observable signatures that astronomers could eventually detect.

Researchers are also using these methods to investigate phenomena such as cosmic strings, primordial black holes, bubble collisions, and the turbulent period known as preheating that may have followed cosmic inflation. Many of these processes could leave traces in gravitational waves or subtle patterns within the cosmic microwave background, creating potential opportunities for future observational tests.

BS Bio—Physiology & Exercise - Affordable In-State Tuition - Salem U Biology in Physiology
BS Bio—Physiology & Exercise - Affordable In-State Tuition - Salem U Biology in Physiology
Salem University
Salem University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Bridging Two Scientific Communities
The review highlights another challenge beyond the technical aspects of the simulations themselves. Historically, cosmologists and numerical relativists have often worked in separate research communities despite sharing common interests in understanding gravity and cosmic evolution.

The authors argue that stronger collaboration between these fields could accelerate progress on some of cosmology’s deepest mysteries. Numerical relativists possess expertise in solving Einstein’s equations under extreme conditions, while cosmologists bring detailed knowledge of observational data and theoretical models describing the universe.

Lim emphasized the importance of building that connection. “We hope to actually develop that overlap between cosmology and numerical relativity so that numerical relativists who are interested in using their techniques to explore cosmological problems can go ahead and do it,” says Lim. “And cosmologists who are interested in solving some of the questions they cannot solve, can use numerical relativity.”

Such collaboration may prove essential as simulations become increasingly sophisticated and computational resources continue to expand. The ability to combine theoretical predictions with observational evidence could help transform long-standing philosophical questions into scientifically testable hypotheses.

What Tennessee school board's 2025 policy change means for Pride flag displays
Story by Joey Esposito • 1mo • 5 min read


An American flag is shown in front of a rainbow. K.C. Alfred / The San Diego Union-Tribune via Getty Images
An American flag is shown in front of a rainbow. K.C. Alfred / The San Diego Union-Tribune via Getty Images

Claim:

A Tennessee school board voted to enact a policy that banned LGBTQ+ Pride flags and allowed only the United States flag or Tennessee state flag to hang in public schools.

Rating:

Mostly True (About this rating?)

What's True:

The Tullahoma City Schools Board of Education voted to change the district's policy regarding flags and other displays such as "banners, pennants, digital signs, etc." in October 2025. The policy effectively restricted any displays to that of the American flag or Tennessee flag unless "directly related to the curriculum or other school-sanctioned event or program."

 
What's False:

The policy does not call for a ban or removal of Pride flags specifically (although the issue stemmed from complaints about a teacher who displayed a Pride flag in their classroom and was explicitly mentioned as the cause of the discussion within the meeting during which the policy was voted on).

 
A rumor that a school board in Tennessee voted in favor of removing LGBTQ+ Pride flags from public schools swirled online in July 2026. 

People on social media posted about the alleged policy change and claimed the school board voted to allow only flags for the United States and the state of Tennessee. 


View this post on Instagram


In sum, the claim was mostly true. During a Tullahoma City Schools Board of Education meeting on Oct. 21, 2025, the board voted to enact a policy that effectively restricted any displays of flags to the American flag or Tennessee state flag unless "directly related to the curriculum or other school-sanctioned event or program."

The policy does not include language calling for a ban or removal of Pride flags specifically. However, the issue stemmed from complaints about a teacher who displayed a Pride flag in their classroom and was explicitly mentioned as the cause of the discussion during the board meeting in which the policy was voted on.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

During the meeting, which is available to view on the school board's website, the policy passed unanimously. The voting on what is referred to as "Board Policy 4.400" can be seen at the 1:22:00 mark. 

The policy, which was read during the board meeting at the 1:07:00 mark, stated in full: 

In order to regulate flags and other displays (ex., banners, pennants, digital signs, etc.) on any/all campuses within Tullahoma City Schools and to ensure a conducive and respectful educational environment, the following guidelines have been established:

1. No flag or other displays (ex., banners, pennants, digital signs, etc.) other than that of the United States of America and state of Tennessee may be displayed in or on Tullahoma City Schools buildings, including classrooms, unless it is an official country, state, TCS school, higher education, post secondary institution, or United States military branch flag used solely for educational purposes.

2. Any other flags or other displays (ex., banners, pennants, digital signs, etc.), including but not limited to political, cultural, or personal, are prohibited from being displayed in or on Tullahoma City Schools buildings unless it is directly related to the curriculum or other school-sanctioned event or program.

3. The American Flag and State flags shall be displayed in accordance with proper flag etiquette as outlined by the United States Flag Code and applicable state regulations. 

4. The display of flags shall be maintained in good condition and replaced when necessary to ensure proper representation and respect.

5. Any requests for exceptions to this policy must be submitted in writing to the principal/designee for consideration. Organization, club, team sponsors or coaches must submit for exceptions to display any flags connected to their team, club or organization. Within this written request, a clear image of the flag or other display shall be provided, along with clear justification for the requested exception, how it connects directly to club, organization, team or classroom, and the duration for the display.

6. If the principal/designee denies the request, this can be appealed to the Director of Schools/Designee within five business days of the original denial. The Director of Schools/Designee will respond to the appeal request in writing with a decision within five business days. The Director of Schools/Designee's decision will be final. 

7. Any approved request may be revoked by the principal/designee in the event of significant or material disruption within the school day.

The Superintendent, in collaboration with school administrators, shall oversee the implementation of this policy across all Tullahoma City Schools' buildings. School personnel shall ensure compliance with this policy and address any violations promptly and appropriately. Educational initiatives may be undertaken to inform students, staff, and the broader school community about the significance and proper display of the American Flag and Tennessee State flag, as well as the rationale behind this policy, if applicable.

Our universe has an evil twin. Scientists say it’s the reason matter exists.
Story by Darren Orf • 4mo • 3 min read


A mirror universe may preserve laws of physics that appear violated.
A mirror universe may preserve laws of physics that appear violated.
© Yana Iskayeva - Getty Images
Here’s what you’ll learn when you read this story:

Scientists have long been puzzled by why matter dominated antimatter in the early universe, when both should have been created equally and annihilated each other.
A new study suggests that a mirror universe may have formed alongside ours in the Big Bang, preserving laws of physics that appear to be violated locally.
The authors found that the same matter-antimatter imbalance likely occurred in both universes during cosmic inflation.
The standard model of particle physics has done wonders for humanity’s never-ending quest to understand the universe and our place within it, but it still leaves some pretty big, glaring questions unanswered. And one of those big questions is arguably the most important of them all: Why is there something rather than nothing?

BS Bio—Physiology & Exercise - Affordable In-State Tuition - Prepare for Further Study
BS Bio—Physiology & Exercise - Affordable In-State Tuition - Prepare for Further Study
Salem University
Salem University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
The conundrum at the heart of this question is matter-antimatter (or baryon) asymmetry, which states that in the early Universe, matter and antimatter should’ve been created in equal amounts. Because these particles annihilate each other and convert their mass to pure energy, the universe should have no matter in it. But of course, you, I, and everything we can sense around us all suggest that’s definitely not what happened.

One way to explain this imbalance is simple—just violate the known rules. These rules are known as charge conjugation (C), parity transformation (P), and time reversal (T), or CPT, and they govern how the universe behaves at all locations and at all times.

Well, at almost all times. The idea is that maybe, at some point in the chaos of the early Universe, a CPT violation could have cause dasymmetry in mesons between matter and antimatter, potentially producing one extra matter particle in every billion.

Related video: Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed (Sciencephile the AI)
View on Watch
View on Watch

Sciencephile the AI
Scientists found a particle 6 times more powerful than physics allowed
Hello mortals.
Current Time 
0:00
/
Duration 
21:10
0
Now, a new study published in the The European Physical Journal C argues that local violations of CPT might have been possible in the early Universe because we’re not actually looking at the entire picture. In other words, the Big Bang may have created a mirror universe alongside our own—one with opposite spatial orientations and reversed time coordinates, where time effectively runs backwards relative to ours. Together, the two universes preserve CPT symmetry as a whole, but within each individual universe, those supposedly immutable laws appear to be locally violated.

“The combined CPT symmetry is widely regarded as exact, and its violation would necessarily indicate physics beyond the Standard Model (SM) of particle physics,” the authors wrote. “We propose that the Big Bang generated […] a pair of universes with opposite orientations, and that CPT symmetry may have been locally violated during the earliest stages of cosmic evolution.”

The LA Recording School - A Division of LA Film School - Music Production Degree Online
The LA Recording School - A Division of LA Film School - Music Production Degree Online
Los Angeles Film School
Los Angeles Film School
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
The authors argue that this local symmetry breaking would have created enough imbalance in the early Universe’s inflaton field to produce the matter-antimatter asymmetry we observe today. (The inflation field is the hypothetical scalar quantum field thought to have driven the universe’s expansion faster than the speed of light during the first moments after the Big Bang). When the authors plugged the numbers into these fields, they found the expected asymmetry in both universes at roughly one part per billion—enough to kick-start a universe dominated by matter (or antimatter).

“A mass difference between the inflaton and its corresponding anti-inflaton field can lead to unequal particle–antiparticle production rates during reheating,” the authors wrote. “The paired-universe model preserves the geometric integrity of spacetime symmetries while providing a natural setting in which CPT symmetry may be globally conserved but locally broken.”

Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities
Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities
Rowan University
Rowan University
·
Sponsored
call to action icon
Is there any way to prove this dual-universe theory? Well, the authors suggest that primordial gravitational waves (generated by this inflationary period) or neutrino asymmetries could carry a fingerprint of these CPT asymmetries from the early universe. The theory could also have implications for how we understand other, longstanding cosmological mysteries, such as dark matter and dark energy.

What Tennessee school board's 2025 policy change means for Pride flag displays
Story by Joey Esposito • 1mo • 5 min read


An American flag is shown in front of a rainbow. K.C. Alfred / The San Diego Union-Tribune via Getty Images
An American flag is shown in front of a rainbow. K.C. Alfred / The San Diego Union-Tribune via Getty Images

Claim:

A Tennessee school board voted to enact a policy that banned LGBTQ+ Pride flags and allowed only the United States flag or Tennessee state flag to hang in public schools.

Rating:

Mostly True (About this rating?)

What's True:

The Tullahoma City Schools Board of Education voted to change the district's policy regarding flags and other displays such as "banners, pennants, digital signs, etc." in October 2025. The policy effectively restricted any displays to that of the American flag or Tennessee flag unless "directly related to the curriculum or other school-sanctioned event or program."

 
What's False:

The policy does not call for a ban or removal of Pride flags specifically (although the issue stemmed from complaints about a teacher who displayed a Pride flag in their classroom and was explicitly mentioned as the cause of the discussion within the meeting during which the policy was voted on).

 
A rumor that a school board in Tennessee voted in favor of removing LGBTQ+ Pride flags from public schools swirled online in July 2026. 

People on social media posted about the alleged policy change and claimed the school board voted to allow only flags for the United States and the state of Tennessee. 


View this post on Instagram


In sum, the claim was mostly true. During a Tullahoma City Schools Board of Education meeting on Oct. 21, 2025, the board voted to enact a policy that effectively restricted any displays of flags to the American flag or Tennessee state flag unless "directly related to the curriculum or other school-sanctioned event or program."

The policy does not include language calling for a ban or removal of Pride flags specifically. However, the issue stemmed from complaints about a teacher who displayed a Pride flag in their classroom and was explicitly mentioned as the cause of the discussion during the board meeting in which the policy was voted on.

Expand article logo  Continue reading

During the meeting, which is available to view on the school board's website, the policy passed unanimously. The voting on what is referred to as "Board Policy 4.400" can be seen at the 1:22:00 mark. 

The policy, which was read during the board meeting at the 1:07:00 mark, stated in full: 

In order to regulate flags and other displays (ex., banners, pennants, digital signs, etc.) on any/all campuses within Tullahoma City Schools and to ensure a conducive and respectful educational environment, the following guidelines have been established:

1. No flag or other displays (ex., banners, pennants, digital signs, etc.) other than that of the United States of America and state of Tennessee may be displayed in or on Tullahoma City Schools buildings, including classrooms, unless it is an official country, state, TCS school, higher education, post secondary institution, or United States military branch flag used solely for educational purposes.

2. Any other flags or other displays (ex., banners, pennants, digital signs, etc.), including but not limited to political, cultural, or personal, are prohibited from being displayed in or on Tullahoma City Schools buildings unless it is directly related to the curriculum or other school-sanctioned event or program.

3. The American Flag and State flags shall be displayed in accordance with proper flag etiquette as outlined by the United States Flag Code and applicable state regulations. 

4. The display of flags shall be maintained in good condition and replaced when necessary to ensure proper representation and respect.

5. Any requests for exceptions to this policy must be submitted in writing to the principal/designee for consideration. Organization, club, team sponsors or coaches must submit for exceptions to display any flags connected to their team, club or organization. Within this written request, a clear image of the flag or other display shall be provided, along with clear justification for the requested exception, how it connects directly to club, organization, team or classroom, and the duration for the display.

6. If the principal/designee denies the request, this can be appealed to the Director of Schools/Designee within five business days of the original denial. The Director of Schools/Designee will respond to the appeal request in writing with a decision within five business days. The Director of Schools/Designee's decision will be final. 

7. Any approved request may be revoked by the principal/designee in the event of significant or material disruption within the school day.

The Superintendent, in collaboration with school administrators, shall oversee the implementation of this policy across all Tullahoma City Schools' buildings. School personnel shall ensure compliance with this policy and address any violations promptly and appropriately. Educational initiatives may be undertaken to inform students, staff, and the broader school community about the significance and proper display of the American Flag and Tennessee State flag, as well as the rationale behind this policy, if applicable.

Study links Sun’s lithium mystery to possible planet engulfment
Ancient cosmic meal?: Researchers propose the Sun engulfed a super-Earth billions of years ago, leaving lasting chemical 'fingerprints' in its interior.

Explaining solar puzzles: The model could resolve discrepancies in the Sun’s convection zone depth and unusually low surface lithium levels.

Proof still pending: Scientists caution the idea is plausible but unconfirmed, urging further helioseismic studies to detect independent evidence.



Study links Sun’s lithium mystery to possible planet engulfment
Hypothesis of a devoured super-Earth
Astronomer Mutlu Yildiz and colleagues modeled scenarios in which the young Sun consumed a rocky planet five to ten times Earth's mass. Their simulations, using the MESA stellar evolution code, suggest such an event could reconcile long-standing mismatches between solar models and helioseismic data, particularly regarding the Sun’s convection zone depth and sound-speed structure. The ingestion could also explain the Sun’s notable surface lithium depletion compared to similar stars. IFLScience +4
Scenario
Estimated Planet Mass
Predicted Solar Effects
Preferred Model
5–10 Earth masses
Matches Sun's convection zone depth, sound-speed structure, and low surface lithium
Alternative Accretion Histories
Varied, including 5.6 Earth masses
Partial improvement in helioseismic and surface abundance properties
Extreme Scenario
Outside 5–10 Earth masses range
Less consistent with observed solar interior and lithium depletion
Futurism +5
Connection to missing super-Earths
Super-Earths are common in exoplanet surveys, yet none exist in our solar system. The study builds on earlier research suggesting one or more such planets may have formed inside Mercury’s orbit and later spiraled into the Sun. If this happened, it could explain their absence today and align with the modeled chemical and structural signatures in the Sun’s interior. The authors stress the scenario is plausible but not proven, pending observational confirmation. IFLScience +4
Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities

Rowan University

Ad Choice
Chemical fingerprints as evidence
The proposed engulfment would have enriched regions beneath the Sun’s convection zone with heavy elements, altering energy transport and internal structure. Lithium, more abundant in planets than in stars, serves as a key tracer: the Sun’s low surface lithium could be consistent with engulfing a lithium-poor super-Earth under specific mixing conditions. Detecting such chemical anomalies through helioseismology or other techniques could provide independent support for the hypothesis. Eurasia Review +4
Next steps for verification
Researchers aim to identify the predicted structural and chemical signatures independently, using precise helioseismic measurements of the Sun’s interior. Comparative studies with other stars showing unusual lithium enrichment or depletion could refine the engulfment model. The team acknowledges that alternative explanations remain viable, and future work must test whether planetary engulfment better fits the data than competing scenarios. Eurasia Review +4

Negative energy in physics and why it matters
Defining the concept: Negative energy means energy density lower than a chosen zero point, such as the vacuum, and can occur in quantum fields, bound systems, or relativistic contexts.

Where it appears: Observed in the Casimir effect, inferred in Hawking radiation, and generated in squeezed light states, it also features in the Penrose process around rotating black holes.

Why it matters: It is a theoretical requirement for exotic spacetime geometries like traversable wormholes and warp drives, but quantum limits make practical use highly challenging.



Negative energy in physics and why it matters
Defining negative energy across theories
In classical and relativistic physics, negative energy often describes bound systems whose total energy is less than that of separated components, like planets in orbit with negative gravitational potential energy. In quantum field theory, it refers to local energy densities below the vacuum level, as in the Casimir effect or squeezed states. In relativistic quantum mechanics, negative energy solutions to equations like Dirac’s are reinterpreted as antiparticles, avoiding instability. grokipedia.com +2
Observed phenomena from lab to cosmos
The Casimir effect shows negative energy density between closely spaced conducting plates due to restricted vacuum fluctuations, producing a measurable attraction. In Hawking radiation, one particle of a virtual pair falls into a black hole with negative energy relative to infinity, reducing the hole’s mass. Squeezed light in quantum optics creates alternating regions of positive and negative energy density by reducing quantum noise in one field quadrature below the vacuum level. Los Angeles Times +2
Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities
Rowan University
Rowan University
·
Sponsored
Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities
Ad Choice
Theoretical applications in spacetime engineering
Negative energy is central to speculative constructs like traversable wormholes, which require it to hold the throat open against collapse, and Alcubierre warp drives, which need it to create a spacetime bubble for apparent faster-than-light travel. While quantum effects can produce tiny amounts, the quantities needed for macroscopic structures are far beyond current capability, and quantum inequalities limit both magnitude and duration. Los Angeles Times +2
Limits, constraints, and misconceptions
Quantum inequalities derived in the 1990s bound the amount and persistence of negative energy, preventing violations of causality or thermodynamics. These limits mean that while negative energy is real in small, transient forms, it cannot be stockpiled for unlimited use. Outside physics, the term is often misapplied to emotions or metaphysical claims, which are unrelated to the precise scientific meaning. EatingWell +2

Negative energy in physics and why it matters
Defining the concept: Negative energy means energy density lower than a chosen zero point, such as the vacuum, and can occur in quantum fields, bound systems, or relativistic contexts.

Where it appears: Observed in the Casimir effect, inferred in Hawking radiation, and generated in squeezed light states, it also features in the Penrose process around rotating black holes.

Why it matters: It is a theoretical requirement for exotic spacetime geometries like traversable wormholes and warp drives, but quantum limits make practical use highly challenging.



Negative energy in physics and why it matters
Defining negative energy across theories
In classical and relativistic physics, negative energy often describes bound systems whose total energy is less than that of separated components, like planets in orbit with negative gravitational potential energy. In quantum field theory, it refers to local energy densities below the vacuum level, as in the Casimir effect or squeezed states. In relativistic quantum mechanics, negative energy solutions to equations like Dirac’s are reinterpreted as antiparticles, avoiding instability. grokipedia.com +2
Observed phenomena from lab to cosmos
The Casimir effect shows negative energy density between closely spaced conducting plates due to restricted vacuum fluctuations, producing a measurable attraction. In Hawking radiation, one particle of a virtual pair falls into a black hole with negative energy relative to infinity, reducing the hole’s mass. Squeezed light in quantum optics creates alternating regions of positive and negative energy density by reducing quantum noise in one field quadrature below the vacuum level. Los Angeles Times +2
Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities
Rowan University
Rowan University
·
Sponsored
Rowan University - Geology Bachelor's Program - Scholarship Opportunities
Ad Choice
Theoretical applications in spacetime engineering
Negative energy is central to speculative constructs like traversable wormholes, which require it to hold the throat open against collapse, and Alcubierre warp drives, which need it to create a spacetime bubble for apparent faster-than-light travel. While quantum effects can produce tiny amounts, the quantities needed for macroscopic structures are far beyond current capability, and quantum inequalities limit both magnitude and duration. Los Angeles Times +2
Limits, constraints, and misconceptions
Quantum inequalities derived in the 1990s bound the amount and persistence of negative energy, preventing violations of causality or thermodynamics. These limits mean that while negative energy is real in small, transient forms, it cannot be stockpiled for unlimited use. Outside physics, the term is often misapplied to emotions or metaphysical claims, which are unrelated to the precise scientific meaning. EatingWell +2

Fauci didn't cover up data supposedly showing COVID-19 vaccine had 82% miscarriage rate
Story by Rae Deng • 1mo • 8 min read


Dr. Anthony Fauci, an older white man with cropped white hair in a blue suit. . Maxine Wallace/The Washington Post via Getty Images
Dr. Anthony Fauci, an older white man with cropped white hair in a blue suit. . Maxine Wallace/The Washington Post via Getty Images

Claim:

Texts prove Dr. Anthony Fauci, who helped lead the United States' COVID-19 response, covered up data indicating that 82% of pregnant women who received an mRNA COVID-19 vaccine in their first trimester miscarried.

Rating:

False (About this rating?)

Context:

The rate of miscarriage among pregnant women who received the COVID-19 vaccine is not 82% — that figure comes from a misinterpretation of a 2021 study. Studies have consistently shown the rate of miscarriage among vaccinated women is in line with rates among the broader population.

 
In August 2026, anti-vaccine advocates claimed online that Dr. Anthony Fauci, the former presidential medical adviser, covered up credible data showing a miscarriage rate of more than 80% among pregnant women who received the COVID-19 vaccine in their first trimester. 

The claim circulated on X, Facebook (screenshott) and Instagram after Sens. Rand Paul, R-Ky., and Ron Johnson, R-Wis., both prominent Fauci critics, released an apparent text message from Fauci that said the second COVID-19 shot "theoretically could be associated with miscarriage in the 1st trimester." 

"They said the Covid vaccine had a 12.6% miscarriage rate for pregnant women but it was actually 82%," one X post said. "Fauci and his co-conspirators need to be thrown in jail."


Fauci didn't cover up data supposedly showing COVID-19 vaccine had 82% miscarriage rate
Fauci didn't cover up data supposedly showing COVID-19 vaccine had 82% miscarriage rate
(X users DC_Draino, KatiePavlich)

Posts called for Fauci's imprisonment, alleging the above text showed that Fauci, who helped lead the nation's COVID-19 pandemic response, knew the COVID-19 vaccine could harm pregnancies but still told pregnant people to get vaccinated. 

We have rated this claim as false. 

Expand article logo  Continue reading

These posts have drawn an incorrect conclusion that relies on flawed data. Multiple studies have shown that pregnant people who receive a COVID-19 vaccine are not at an increased risk of miscarriage or pregnancy complications. As numerous fact-checking organizations determined in 2021 — and as we have independently verified — the 82% statistic is a gross misrepresentation of data released in an April 2021 study. 

Meanwhile, the apparent text from Fauci at the center of the claim was dated January 2021, before researchers were done collecting data for the study. That means Fauci could not have been referring to its supposed findings.

The Water That Defies Gravity
©Photo by Lester Hine on Unsplash
Liquid molecules are supposed to fall downward when poured onto an open surface. According to physicists at Harvard University, researchers have observed water droplets climbing vertically up highly specialized copper sheets. The movement is silent. This strange action occurs when micro-textures on the metal manipulate capillary forces to override gravity. But another biological anomaly is actively confusing researchers on land.

The Metal-Eating Tree
©Photo by Declan Sun on Unsplash
Most forest plants avoid absorbing heavy metals from local soils to protect their cells. According to botanical reports from the University of Queensland, a unique rainforest tree actively sucks up toxic nickel until its sap turns bright green. It is highly toxic. This strange plant can hold massive amounts of metal without suffering any biological damage. But the ocean holds a physical mystery that is even harder to explain.

In August 2026, anti-vaccine advocates claimed online that Dr. Anthony Fauci, the former presidential medical adviser, covered up credible data showing a miscarriage rate of more than 80% among pregnant women who received the COVID-19 vaccine in their first trimester. 

The claim circulated on X, Facebook (screenshott) and Instagram after Sens. Rand Paul, R-Ky., and Ron Johnson, R-Wis., both prominent Fauci critics, released an apparent text message from Fauci that said the second COVID-19 shot "theoretically could be associated with miscarriage in the 1st trimester." 

"They said the Covid vaccine had a 12.6% miscarriage rate for pregnant women but it was actually 82%," one X post said. "Fauci and his co-conspirators need to be thrown in jail."


Fauci didn't cover up data supposedly showing COVID-19 vaccine had 82% miscarriage rate
Fauci didn't cover up data supposedly showing COVID-19 vaccine had 82% miscarriage rate
(X users DC_Draino, KatiePavlich)

Posts called for Fauci's imprisonment, alleging the above text showed that Fauci, who helped lead the nation's COVID-19 pandemic response, knew the COVID-19 vaccine could harm pregnancies but still told pregnant people to get vaccinated. 

We have rated this claim as false. 

Expand article logo  Continue reading

These posts have drawn an incorrect conclusion that relies on flawed data. Multiple studies have shown that pregnant people who receive a COVID-19 vaccine are not at an increased risk of miscarriage or pregnancy complications. As numerous fact-checking organizations determined in 2021 — and as we have independently verified — the 82% statistic is a gross misrepresentation of data released in an April 2021 study. 

Meanwhile, the apparent text from Fauci at the center of the claim was dated January 2021, before researchers were done collecting data for the study. That means Fauci could not have been referring to its supposed findings.
